Looking back, there were signs when I was little, even if I didn’t think of it as restlessness or difficulty sitting still back then.\n\nLike in elementary school, I remember teachers writing that I was bright, but I’d lose worksheets, miss instructions, and need reminders to finish classwork. So it was kind of this pattern of getting off track or not staying with what I was supposed to be doing. It’s hard for me to separate that from the sitting-still part, because as a kid I wasn’t keeping track of, “Oh, I’m fidgeting,” but the general issue of not being settled and focused was definitely there.\n\nI think there have probably been times when it was more noticeable depending on the setting, like if there’s more pressure to sit and pay attention, but I don’t really think there was a clean period where it completely went away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you interrupted others or blurted out answers before they finished speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly cutting people off, at least not in a really obvious way. Like, I’m not usually the person shouting answers over people or interrupting in a meeting, if that’s what you mean.\n\nBut… I guess there are moments where I realize I started talking before the other person was totally finished, especially if I think I know where they’re going with it. Then I kind of catch myself and feel awkward, like, “Sorry, go ahead.” I don’t know if that happens more than normal, though. It’s not something I’ve really tracked or had people complain about a lot.\n\nSo I’d say maybe occasionally, but I’m not sure I’d call it a major pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself saying things without thinking, like speaking out of turn or saying something you later regret?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” no. I mean, I can think of times where I’ve said something and then afterward been like, “Why did I phrase it that way?” or I worry that it came out more blunt than I meant. But I don’t know if that’s the same as speaking without thinking, exactly.\n\nI’m usually pretty aware of not wanting to say the wrong thing, so I think I tend to hold back more than blurt things out. Like in work situations or with people I don’t know well, I’m usually trying to filter myself. With people I’m comfortable with, maybe I’ll be a little more quick to say something, but I don’t have a big pattern of people telling me I’m always putting my foot in my mouth or interrupting with inappropriate comments.\n\nSo… I guess it happens occasionally, like it probably does for anyone, but I haven’t really noticed it as a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with sitting still, paying attention, or other issues you've mentioned caused problems for you in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work, I guess the main place it’s really becoming a problem is daily life in the sense of my job bleeding into everything else. My supervisor actually put me on performance coaching recently because my claim files are falling behind, and now my accuracy is being questioned too. So it’s not just like, “Oh, I’m a little scattered,” it’s gotten to the point where someone else is formally noticing it and documenting it.\n\nAnd that’s been stressing me out a lot, because I was trying to move toward the senior adjuster track eventually, and now I’m worried I could lose that chance, or honestly even be seen as unreliable. I know you asked outside of work, but it affects me after work too because I’ll come home still thinking about the files I didn’t finish or whether I made mistakes, and it kind of hangs over me. So relationships-wise, I don’t know that I’ve had some huge blowup because of it, but daily life, definitely, because it’s hard to shut off the worry when I feel like I’m falling behind and people are starting to question whether I can handle the role.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships, such as with friends or family, because of your difficulties with restlessness or staying focused?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to a clear pattern with friends or family, honestly. Like, no one has sat me down and said, “You never pay attention to me,” or “You’re too restless to be around,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, sometimes I worry I’m not as present as I should be, especially if someone is telling me something and I’m trying to follow it but then I realize I may not have taken in every detail. But I don’t know if that has actually caused relationship problems, or if that’s just me being self-conscious after the fact. With family, I think they mostly just see me as kind of distracted sometimes, but it hasn’t turned into big arguments or people pulling away from me, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nSo I’d say… not really, or not in a way I can clearly identify. It’s more that I worry about it than that I have concrete examples.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with restlessness or staying focused as a child, similar to what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I don’t think this is completely new. At the time I don’t think I understood it that way, because when you’re a kid you just kind of think school is school and you’re doing whatever everyone else is doing. But I do remember in elementary school, teachers would write things like that I was bright, or that I understood the material, but then there would always be comments about me losing worksheets or not turning things in, or needing reminders to finish classwork.\n\nAnd I remember missing instructions sometimes—not because I was trying to ignore anyone, but I’d realize everyone else had started doing something and I was still like, “Wait, what are we supposed to be doing?” Then I’d feel embarrassed and either ask someone next to me or try to figure it out from watching them. It wasn’t that I couldn’t do the work, exactly. It was more like keeping track of the paper, the steps, and actually finishing it during the time we were supposed to.\n\nSo, yeah… I guess similar in the staying-focused sense. I don’t know that I would have called it a major issue back then, but the pattern was there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to complete a project or achieve a goal because of your difficulties with restlessness, focus, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the biggest example right now is my goal at work. I was trying to move toward the senior adjuster track, like that was kind of the next step I had in mind, and now it feels like I may have derailed that.\n\nMy supervisor put me on performance coaching because my claim files are falling behind, and it’s not just the volume anymore — my accuracy is being questioned too. So it’s hard not to see that as me not being able to complete what I’m supposed to complete, or at least not at the level I need to. I keep thinking, if I can’t keep the files current and clean, then why would they trust me with a senior role?\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say I’ve totally “failed” at the goal yet, because I guess it’s still technically possible, but it definitely feels like I’m losing that path. And that’s really frustrating, because I’m not trying to slack off or anything. I’ll sit there meaning to get through everything, and then by the end I’m still behind, and now it’s become formal enough that my supervisor is documenting it. That makes it feel a lot more real.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced restlessness or difficulty sitting still in multiple different environments, such as at home, in social situations, or during leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so, but the clearest place I notice it is in structured settings, like trainings or longer team meetings at work. If we’re sitting there for a while, I’m usually doing something without even fully realizing it at first—bouncing my leg under the table, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair, changing how I’m sitting, that kind of thing.\n\nAnd I’ll be trying to look like I’m paying attention, because I don’t want to seem rude or checked out, but physically I feel like I have to keep moving a little bit. It’s not like I’m getting up and pacing around the room or anything dramatic. It’s more subtle, but it’s pretty constant once I notice it. Sometimes I’ll catch myself clicking the pen and then stop because I’m worried it’s annoying someone, and then a minute later I’m bouncing my leg instead.\n\nSo, yeah, in those longer meetings or trainings especially, sitting still feels harder than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit any of these restless or inattentive behaviors before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Like the teacher would explain something, and then suddenly everyone else would be working and I’d be sitting there trying to figure out what I was supposed to be doing. Yeah… I guess I’d say it’s been pretty consistent, especially in situations where I’m expected to sit still and pay attention for a long stretch. The work examples are the easiest for me to point to now, like trainings or longer team meetings. I’ll be sitting there trying to look engaged, and meanwhile my leg is bouncing under the table, or I’m clicking a pen, or I keep shifting around in the chair like I can’t quite get comfortable.

It’s not always something I’m consciously choosing to do, either. A lot of the time I notice it after the fact, like I’ll realize, “Oh, I’ve been tapping my foot this whole time,” or I’ll catch myself clicking the pen and worry someone can hear it. Then I’ll stop, but usually I just end up moving in some other way, like adjusting my posture or fidgeting with something else.

So, yes, in that kind of environment it’s been pretty steady over time. It’s not like I’m jumping up and leaving the room, but the sitting-still part feels like it takes effort, even when I’m trying to look focused.

If I’m in a store I’m usually just trying to get what I need and leave, not climbing on shelves or anything. So for that specific thing, I’d say no, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience restlessness or have trouble sitting still in situations outside of work, like when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it shows up outside of work too, but I notice it most clearly when I’m in those situations where I’m supposed to sit still and seem focused. Like, the same kind of thing happens in longer trainings or team meetings — I’ll be bouncing my leg, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair, and I don’t always realize how much I’m doing it until I either hear the pen clicking or I notice my leg moving.\n\nOutside of work, if I’m sitting with friends or at home, it’s a little less obvious maybe, because there isn’t that same pressure to look professional. But I still tend to move around in my seat or adjust a lot. It’s not like I’m jumping up and running around, it’s more this constant small movement, like I can sit there, but staying completely still feels weirdly uncomfortable after a while.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience similar restlessness or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, looking back. I don’t know if I would’ve described it as “restlessness” at the time, because when you’re a kid you don’t really have that language for it, but it doesn’t feel like this is brand new for me.\n\nIn elementary school, my teachers used to write stuff on report cards like I was bright, but I’d lose worksheets or miss instructions, or I needed reminders to finish classwork. I remember that kind of pattern more than, like, a specific memory of me not being able to sit in the chair. It was more that I’d get off track, or I’d think I knew what we were supposed to do and then realize I missed part of the directions, or I’d have the paper somewhere and then suddenly it was gone.\n\nSo… yes, I think there were signs when I was younger. It wasn’t just something that started in adulthood or just because of my job now.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more trouble following rules or behaving in a classroom setting when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t remember being the kid who was constantly in trouble or getting sent to the principal or anything like that. Like, I don’t think I had a reputation for being badly behaved, at least not that I know of.\n\nIf you mean following classroom rules in the sense of staying with the lesson, turning things in the right way, waiting for instructions, that kind of thing… I might’ve had trouble there, but I don’t remember it as “breaking rules” exactly. It was more like teachers having to redirect me or remind me what I was supposed to be doing. But I don’t have a clear memory of, like, defiance or acting out in class.\n\nSo I guess I’d say no, not in a major behavior-problem way. Or at least I’m not sure — I’d probably remember more if it had been a big issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these kinds of restlessness and difficulty sitting still consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were better or worse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s been pretty consistent, at least in the sense that it doesn’t feel like something that suddenly appeared recently. Looking back, there were signs when I was little, even if I didn’t think of it as restlessness or difficulty sitting still back then.\n\nLike in elementary school, I remember teachers writing that I was bright, but I’d lose worksheets, miss instructions, and need reminders to finish classwork. So it was kind of this pattern of getting off track or not staying with what I was supposed to be doing. It’s hard for me to separate that from the sitting-still part, because as a kid I wasn’t keeping track of, “Oh, I’m fidgeting,” but the general issue of not being settled and focused was definitely there.\n\nI think there have probably been times when it was more noticeable depending on the setting, like if there’s more pressure to sit and pay attention, but I don’t really think there was a clean period where it completely went away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you interrupted others or blurted out answers before they finished speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly cutting people off, at least not in a really obvious way. Like, I’m not usually the person shouting answers over people or interrupting in a meeting, if that’s what you mean.\n\nBut… I guess there are moments where I realize I started talking before the other person was totally finished, especially if I think I know where they’re going with it. Then I kind of catch myself and feel awkward, like, “Sorry, go ahead.” I don’t know if that happens more than normal, though. It’s not something I’ve really tracked or had people complain about a lot.\n\nSo I’d say maybe occasionally, but I’m not sure I’d call it a major pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself saying things without thinking, like speaking out of turn or saying something you later regret?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” no. I mean, I can think of times where I’ve said something and then afterward been like, “Why did I phrase it that way?” or I worry that it came out more blunt than I meant. But I don’t know if that’s the same as speaking without thinking, exactly.\n\nI’m usually pretty aware of not wanting to say the wrong thing, so I think I tend to hold back more than blurt things out. Like in work situations or with people I don’t know well, I’m usually trying to filter myself. With people I’m comfortable with, maybe I’ll be a little more quick to say something, but I don’t have a big pattern of people telling me I’m always putting my foot in my mouth or interrupting with inappropriate comments.\n\nSo… I guess it happens occasionally, like it probably does for anyone, but I haven’t really noticed it as a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with sitting still, paying attention, or other issues you've mentioned caused problems for you in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work, I guess the main place it’s really becoming a problem is daily life in the sense of my job bleeding into everything else. My supervisor actually put me on performance coaching recently because my claim files are falling behind, and now my accuracy is being questioned too. So it’s not just like, “Oh, I’m a little scattered,” it’s gotten to the point where someone else is formally noticing it and documenting it.\n\nAnd that’s been stressing me out a lot, because I was trying to move toward the senior adjuster track eventually, and now I’m worried I could lose that chance, or honestly even be seen as unreliable. I know you asked outside of work, but it affects me after work too because I’ll come home still thinking about the files I didn’t finish or whether I made mistakes, and it kind of hangs over me. So relationships-wise, I don’t know that I’ve had some huge blowup because of it, but daily life, definitely, because it’s hard to shut off the worry when I feel like I’m falling behind and people are starting to question whether I can handle the role.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your personal relationships, such as with friends or family, because of your difficulties with restlessness or staying focused?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to a clear pattern with friends or family, honestly. Like, no one has sat me down and said, “You never pay attention to me,” or “You’re too restless to be around,” or anything like that.\n\nI mean, sometimes I worry I’m not as present as I should be, especially if someone is telling me something and I’m trying to follow it but then I realize I may not have taken in every detail. But I don’t know if that has actually caused relationship problems, or if that’s just me being self-conscious after the fact. With family, I think they mostly just see me as kind of distracted sometimes, but it hasn’t turned into big arguments or people pulling away from me, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nSo I’d say… not really, or not in a way I can clearly identify. It’s more that I worry about it than that I have concrete examples.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with restlessness or staying focused as a child, similar to what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I don’t think this is completely new. At the time I don’t think I understood it that way, because when you’re a kid you just kind of think school is school and you’re doing whatever everyone else is doing. But I do remember in elementary school, teachers would write things like that I was bright, or that I understood the material, but then there would always be comments about me losing worksheets or not turning things in, or needing reminders to finish classwork.\n\nAnd I remember missing instructions sometimes—not because I was trying to ignore anyone, but I’d realize everyone else had started doing something and I was still like, “Wait, what are we supposed to be doing?” Then I’d feel embarrassed and either ask someone next to me or try to figure it out from watching them. It wasn’t that I couldn’t do the work, exactly. It was more like keeping track of the paper, the steps, and actually finishing it during the time we were supposed to.\n\nSo, yeah… I guess similar in the staying-focused sense. I don’t know that I would have called it a major issue back then, but the pattern was there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to complete a project or achieve a goal because of your difficulties with restlessness, focus, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the biggest example right now is my goal at work. I was trying to move toward the senior adjuster track, like that was kind of the next step I had in mind, and now it feels like I may have derailed that.\n\nMy supervisor put me on performance coaching because my claim files are falling behind, and it’s not just the volume anymore — my accuracy is being questioned too. So it’s hard not to see that as me not being able to complete what I’m supposed to complete, or at least not at the level I need to. I keep thinking, if I can’t keep the files current and clean, then why would they trust me with a senior role?\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say I’ve totally “failed” at the goal yet, because I guess it’s still technically possible, but it definitely feels like I’m losing that path. And that’s really frustrating, because I’m not trying to slack off or anything. I’ll sit there meaning to get through everything, and then by the end I’m still behind, and now it’s become formal enough that my supervisor is documenting it. That makes it feel a lot more real.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced restlessness or difficulty sitting still in multiple different environments, such as at home, in social situations, or during leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so, but the clearest place I notice it is in structured settings, like trainings or longer team meetings at work. If we’re sitting there for a while, I’m usually doing something without even fully realizing it at first—bouncing my leg under the table, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair, changing how I’m sitting, that kind of thing.\n\nAnd I’ll be trying to look like I’m paying attention, because I don’t want to seem rude or checked out, but physically I feel like I have to keep moving a little bit. It’s not like I’m getting up and pacing around the room or anything dramatic. It’s more subtle, but it’s pretty constant once I notice it. Sometimes I’ll catch myself clicking the pen and then stop because I’m worried it’s annoying someone, and then a minute later I’m bouncing my leg instead.\n\nSo, yeah, in those longer meetings or trainings especially, sitting still feels harder than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit any of these restless or inattentive behaviors before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I didn’t have language for it then, obviously, but looking back it was there before middle school. In elementary school, I remember my teachers would say I was bright or that I understood the material, but there were always these comments about the practical stuff — like I’d lose worksheets, or not turn things in, or I’d need reminders to finish classwork.\n\nAnd I remember missing instructions sometimes. Like the teacher would explain something, and then suddenly everyone else would be working and I’d be sitting there trying to figure out what I was supposed to be doing. It wasn’t that I didn’t care or couldn’t do the assignment, it was more like I wasn’t keeping track of the steps or the paper or the timing. So yes, I’d say some of the inattentive stuff was definitely showing up before I was 12.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced restlessness or difficulty sitting still in all of these different environments consistently over the years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’d say it’s been pretty consistent, especially in situations where I’m expected to sit still and pay attention for a long stretch. The work examples are the easiest for me to point to now, like trainings or longer team meetings. I’ll be sitting there trying to look engaged, and meanwhile my leg is bouncing under the table, or I’m clicking a pen, or I keep shifting around in the chair like I can’t quite get comfortable.\n\nIt’s not always something I’m consciously choosing to do, either. A lot of the time I notice it after the fact, like I’ll realize, “Oh, I’ve been tapping my foot this whole time,” or I’ll catch myself clicking the pen and worry someone can hear it. Then I’ll stop, but usually I just end up moving in some other way, like adjusting my posture or fidgeting with something else.\n\nSo, yes, in that kind of environment it’s been pretty steady over time. It’s not like I’m jumping up and leaving the room, but the sitting-still part feels like it takes effort, even when I’m trying to look focused.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It wasn’t that I couldn’t do the work, exactly. It was more like keeping track of the paper, the steps, and actually finishing it during the time we were supposed to.\n\nSo, yeah… I guess similar in the staying-focused sense. I don’t know that I would have called it a major issue back then, but the pattern was there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to complete a project or achieve a goal because of your difficulties with restlessness, focus, or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the biggest example right now is my goal at work. I was trying to move toward the senior adjuster track, like that was kind of the next step I had in mind, and now it feels like I may have derailed that.\n\nMy supervisor put me on performance coaching because my claim files are falling behind, and it’s not just the volume anymore — my accuracy is being questioned too. So it’s hard not to see that as me not being able to complete what I’m supposed to complete, or at least not at the level I need to. I keep thinking, if I can’t keep the files current and clean, then why would they trust me with a senior role?\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say I’ve totally “failed” at the goal yet, because I guess it’s still technically possible, but it definitely feels like I’m losing that path. And that’s really frustrating, because I’m not trying to slack off or anything. I’ll sit there meaning to get through everything, and then by the end I’m still behind, and now it’s become formal enough that my supervisor is documenting it. That makes it feel a lot more real.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced restlessness or difficulty sitting still in multiple different environments, such as at home, in social situations, or during leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so, but the clearest place I notice it is in structured settings, like trainings or longer team meetings at work. If we’re sitting there for a while, I’m usually doing something without even fully realizing it at first—bouncing my leg under the table, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair, changing how I’m sitting, that kind of thing.\n\nAnd I’ll be trying to look like I’m paying attention, because I don’t want to seem rude or checked out, but physically I feel like I have to keep moving a little bit. It’s not like I’m getting up and pacing around the room or anything dramatic. It’s more subtle, but it’s pretty constant once I notice it. Sometimes I’ll catch myself clicking the pen and then stop because I’m worried it’s annoying someone, and then a minute later I’m bouncing my leg instead.\n\nSo, yeah, in those longer meetings or trainings especially, sitting still feels harder than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit any of these restless or inattentive behaviors before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I didn’t have language for it then, obviously, but looking back it was there before middle school. In elementary school, I remember my teachers would say I was bright or that I understood the material, but there were always these comments about the practical stuff — like I’d lose worksheets, or not turn things in, or I’d need reminders to finish classwork.\n\nAnd I remember missing instructions sometimes. Like the teacher would explain something, and then suddenly everyone else would be working and I’d be sitting there trying to figure out what I was supposed to be doing. It wasn’t that I didn’t care or couldn’t do the assignment, it was more like I wasn’t keeping track of the steps or the paper or the timing. So yes, I’d say some of the inattentive stuff was definitely showing up before I was 12.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced restlessness or difficulty sitting still in all of these different environments consistently over the years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I’d say it’s been pretty consistent, especially in situations where I’m expected to sit still and pay attention for a long stretch. The work examples are the easiest for me to point to now, like trainings or longer team meetings. I’ll be sitting there trying to look engaged, and meanwhile my leg is bouncing under the table, or I’m clicking a pen, or I keep shifting around in the chair like I can’t quite get comfortable.\n\nIt’s not always something I’m consciously choosing to do, either. A lot of the time I notice it after the fact, like I’ll realize, “Oh, I’ve been tapping my foot this whole time,” or I’ll catch myself clicking the pen and worry someone can hear it. Then I’ll stop, but usually I just end up moving in some other way, like adjusting my posture or fidgeting with something else.\n\nSo, yes, in that kind of environment it’s been pretty steady over time. It’s not like I’m jumping up and leaving the room, but the sitting-still part feels like it takes effort, even when I’m trying to look focused.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely more than 6 months. The work-related problems I’m thinking of have been going on since our department reorganization, which was about 15 months ago now.\n\nIt kind of stands out to me because before that, work had its own issues, but after the reorg the structure changed and things became harder for me to keep up with consistently. So when I think about the stuff that’s been causing problems at work, it’s not recent or just a bad couple of weeks. It’s been over a year at this point — around 15 months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yes. I mean, the biggest place it’s showing up is work, and it’s gotten to the point where it’s not just me feeling stressed internally — other people are noticing it and documenting it.

I’ll be sitting there, aware that I have reviews waiting, and then I’ll just find myself standing up or walking away from my station. And afterward I’m like, why did I do that? I knew I was supposed to keep working. It’s been happening enough that it’s starting to feel like a real problem, and that’s what pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself acting on impulse or doing things without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I think of myself as someone who does reckless things or anything like that. Like, if you mean big impulsive decisions, I don’t really have an example that jumps out.\n\nI guess sometimes I do things before I’ve fully thought them through in the moment, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I’ll realize afterward that I didn’t really pause first. But I don’t know if I’d call it impulsive exactly. Could you maybe give me an example of what kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting at your desk?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I notice it more in situations where I’m expected to just sit still and look engaged, like trainings or longer team meetings. I’ll be trying to pay attention and look like I’m focused, but my leg is bouncing under the table almost the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen without realizing how much I’m doing it.\n\nSometimes I’ll keep shifting around in the chair too, like leaning one way, then sitting back, then adjusting again a minute later. It’s not always that I’m trying to leave right then, but sitting still feels… uncomfortable, I guess. And then I become aware that I’m doing all this movement and try to stop because I don’t want to look distracted, but after a little while I’ll catch myself doing it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d say that’s the main thing I’ve noticed. With my work, there are a lot of details in the claims, like policy numbers and codes and dates and notes, so I do try to be careful because I know mistakes can cause problems.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about whether I make more careless mistakes than other people. Nobody has really framed it to me that way, like “you’re missing details all the time,” at least not that I can remember. Sometimes I’ll look back over something and catch something small before submitting it, but I don’t know if that’s just normal with that kind of work.\n\nSo… I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern of careless mistakes specifically, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself climbing or running in situations where it's not really appropriate, like at work or in a store?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, not literally running around or climbing on things in places where I shouldn’t. At work I’m not, like, getting up and pacing around the office or doing anything that would stand out like that.\n\nMaybe when I was a kid there were times I’d run around more, but as an adult, especially at work or in a store, I know that would be inappropriate, so I don’t really do that. If I’m in a store I’m usually just trying to get what I need and leave, not climbing on shelves or anything. So for that specific thing, I’d say no, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience restlessness or have trouble sitting still in situations outside of work, like when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it shows up outside of work too, but I notice it most clearly when I’m in those situations where I’m supposed to sit still and seem focused. Like, the same kind of thing happens in longer trainings or team meetings — I’ll be bouncing my leg, clicking my pen, shifting around in the chair, and I don’t always realize how much I’m doing it until I either hear the pen clicking or I notice my leg moving.\n\nOutside of work, if I’m sitting with friends or at home, it’s a little less obvious maybe, because there isn’t that same pressure to look professional. But I still tend to move around in my seat or adjust a lot. It’s not like I’m jumping up and running around, it’s more this constant small movement, like I can sit there, but staying completely still feels weirdly uncomfortable after a while.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience similar restlessness or difficulty sitting still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, looking back. I don’t know if I would’ve described it as “restlessness” at the time, because when you’re a kid you don’t really have that language for it, but it doesn’t feel like this is brand new for me.\n\nIn elementary school, my teachers used to write stuff on report cards like I was bright, but I’d lose worksheets or miss instructions, or I needed reminders to finish classwork. I remember that kind of pattern more than, like, a specific memory of me not being able to sit in the chair. It was more that I’d get off track, or I’d think I knew what we were supposed to do and then realize I missed part of the directions, or I’d have the paper somewhere and then suddenly it was gone.\n\nSo… yes, I think there were signs when I was younger. It wasn’t just something that started in adulthood or just because of my job now.\n\nDoctor: Did you have more trouble following rules or behaving in a classroom setting when you were in school?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. It was more like teachers having to redirect me or remind me what I was supposed to be doing. But I don’t have a clear memory of, like, defiance or acting out in class.\n\nSo I guess I’d say no, not in a major behavior-problem way. Or at least I’m not sure — I’d probably remember more if it had been a big issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these kinds of restlessness and difficulty sitting still consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were better or worse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s been pretty consistent, at least in the sense that it doesn’t feel like something that suddenly appeared recently. Looking back, there were signs when I was little, even if I didn’t think of it as restlessness or difficulty sitting still back then.\n\nLike in elementary school, I remember teachers writing that I was bright, but I’d lose worksheets, miss instructions, and need reminders to finish classwork. So it was kind of this pattern of getting off track or not staying with what I was supposed to be doing. It’s hard for me to separate that from the sitting-still part, because as a kid I wasn’t keeping track of, “Oh, I’m fidgeting,” but the general issue of not being settled and focused was definitely there.\n\nI think there have probably been times when it was more noticeable depending on the setting, like if there’s more pressure to sit and pay attention, but I don’t really think there was a clean period where it completely went away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you interrupted others or blurted out answers before they finished speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly cutting people off, at least not in a really obvious way. Like, I’m not usually the person shouting answers over people or interrupting in a meeting, if that’s what you mean.\n\nBut… I guess there are moments where I realize I started talking before the other person was totally finished, especially if I think I know where they’re going with it. But I don’t know if that’s the same as speaking without thinking, exactly.\n\nI’m usually pretty aware of not wanting to say the wrong thing, so I think I tend to hold back more than blurt things out. Like in work situations or with people I don’t know well, I’m usually trying to filter myself. With people I’m comfortable with, maybe I’ll be a little more quick to say something, but I don’t have a big pattern of people telling me I’m always putting my foot in my mouth or interrupting with inappropriate comments.\n\nSo… I guess it happens occasionally, like it probably does for anyone, but I haven’t really noticed it as a major problem.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with sitting still, paying attention, or other issues you've mentioned caused problems for you in your relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, outside of work, I guess the main place it’s really becoming a problem is daily life in the sense of my job bleeding into everything else. My supervisor actually put me on performance coaching recently because my claim files are falling behind, and now my accuracy is being questioned too. So it’s not just like, “Oh, I’m a little scattered,” it’s gotten to the point where someone else is formally noticing it and documenting it.\n\nAnd that’s been stressing me out a lot, because I was trying to move toward the senior adjuster track eventually, and now I’m worried I could lose that chance, or honestly even be seen as unreliable. I know you asked outside of work, but it affects me after work too because I’ll come home still thinking about the files I didn’t finish or whether I made mistakes, and it kind of hangs over me. Then I’ll stop, but usually I just end up moving in some other way, like adjusting my posture or fidgeting with something else.\n\nSo, yes, in that kind of environment it’s been pretty steady over time.
